Failure to Control: Unable to regulate worry despite efforts to stop or distract oneself.\n  - Intrusive Worry: Worries suddenly intrude into the mind and interfere with focus while doing other tasks.\n  - Chaining of Worry: One worry triggers another in a chain reaction, escalating to catastrophic thoughts.\n  - Feeling Keyed Up: Feeling anxious and wound up like a spring, unable to stay still.\n  - Physical Tension: The entire body feels stiff and tense, unable to relax.\n  - Nervousness: Nerves are on edge, being startled easily by minor sounds or stimuli.\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - Duration: 1 year and 12 months\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can specifically answer based on the profile, set matched=true, list related\n  sections/items in matched_sections, and write a short English strategy in answer_strategy\n  on what tone and content the patient should reply with.\n- If the question is not in the profile or can only be inferred, set matched=false and instruct\n  in answer_strategy to respond briefly (e.g., \"I'm not sure\", \"I haven't thought about it\").\n- If patient_turn_index is 1 (patient's first reply), ONLY focus the strategy on the chief complaint\n  (do NOT reveal other symptoms).\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"matched\": true|false, \"matched_sections\": [\"...\"], \"answer_strategy\": \"...\"}\n"

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm coming in today because I've been struggling with constant, uncontrollable worry and anxiety for a long time now. It has been happening almost every day for about one to two years, and I just can't seem to make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms such as restlessness, fatigue, or difficulty sleeping when you're feeling anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel extremely restless and wound up like a spring, to the point where I just can't stay still. My entire body constantly feels incredibly stiff and tense, making it impossible to relax. I'm not as sure about fatigue or sleep issues, but the physical tension and restlessness are definitely very prominent.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find it hard to focus on tasks, make careless mistakes, or interrupt others, and do you often act on impulse or have difficulty waiting your turn?\n\nPatient: I don't really experience things like acting on impulse, interrupting others, or making careless mistakes. However, I do sometimes find it hard to focus on my tasks, but that's just because my mind is constantly filled with worries.\n\nDoctor: When you're having trouble focusing on tasks, is it primarily because your mind is preoccupied with worries, or do you find your attention is easily diverted to non-essential things, like your surroundings or unrelated thoughts?\n\nPatient: It is definitely because worries suddenly pop into my mind and completely take over, rather than me getting distracted by my surroundings. These intrusive thoughts just hijack my brain, making it incredibly hard to focus on whatever task I'm trying to do.\n\nDoctor: How much do these worries and difficulties with focus interfere with your daily life, such as your relationships, work, or social activities?\n\nPatient: They interfere with my daily life a lot because these worries constantly intrude and make it extremely hard to focus on my work. Since my anxiety spreads to almost everything—from my family and finances to my job—it has become completely overwhelming and is really straining my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations, events, or topics that tend to trigger or worsen your anxiety and worries, or are they more free-floating and unpredictable?\n\nPatient: They are definitely more free-floating and unpredictable rather than being triggered by specific situations. My worry just seems to spread across almost every area of my life, from my job and finances to my family's well-being. It feels like my mind is constantly finding new things to worry about, no matter what is actually happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how these worries affect your daily routines and if you've noticed any patterns to when your anxiety is at its worst, such as certain times of the day or when you're under pressure?\n\nPatient: These worries constantly disrupt my daily routine because they suddenly pop into my head, making it incredibly hard to focus on work or family tasks. I don't really have a specific time of day when it's worse since it feels pretty constant almost every day. However, the anxiety definitely peaks whenever I face any uncertainty or make even a minor mistake.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation where you need to concentrate, do you think your mind wanders because you're anxious about something, or do you feel like you're anxious because you're having trouble concentrating?"
  }

My mind definitely wanders because worries suddenly intrude and disrupt my focus while I am trying to concentrate. It is not that I get anxious because I have trouble concentrating, but rather that these sudden, intense worries force their way in and make it impossible to stay on task.

Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm coming in today because I've been struggling with constant, uncontrollable worry and anxiety for a long time now. It has been happening almost every day for about one to two years, and I just can't seem to make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms such as restlessness, fatigue, or difficulty sleeping when you're feeling anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel extremely restless and wound up like a spring, to the point where I just can't stay still. My entire body constantly feels incredibly stiff and tense, making it impossible to relax. I'm not as sure about fatigue or sleep issues, but the physical tension and restlessness are definitely very prominent.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find it hard to focus on tasks, make careless mistakes, or interrupt others, and do you often act on impulse or have difficulty waiting your turn?\n\nPatient: I don't really experience things like acting on impulse, interrupting others, or making careless mistakes. However, I do sometimes find it hard to focus on my tasks, but that's just because my mind is constantly filled with worries.\n\nDoctor: When you're having trouble focusing on tasks, is it primarily because your mind is preoccupied with worries, or do you find your attention is easily diverted to non-essential things, like your surroundings or unrelated thoughts?\n\nPatient: It is definitely because worries suddenly pop into my mind and completely take over, rather than me getting distracted by my surroundings. These intrusive thoughts just hijack my brain, making it incredibly hard to focus on whatever task I'm trying to do.\n\nDoctor: How much do these worries and difficulties with focus interfere with your daily life, such as your relationships, work, or social activities?\n\nPatient: They interfere with my daily life a lot because these worries constantly intrude and make it extremely hard to focus on my work. Since my anxiety spreads to almost everything—from my family and finances to my job—it has become completely overwhelming and is really straining my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations, events, or topics that tend to trigger or worsen your anxiety and worries, or are they more free-floating and unpredictable?\n\nPatient: They are definitely more free-floating and unpredictable rather than being triggered by specific situations. My worry just seems to spread across almost every area of my life, from my job and finances to my family's well-being. It feels like my mind is constantly finding new things to worry about, no matter what is actually happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how these worries affect your daily routines and if you've noticed any patterns to when your anxiety is at its worst, such as certain times of the day or when you're under pressure?\n\nPatient: These worries constantly disrupt my daily routine because they suddenly pop into my head, making it incredibly hard to focus on work or family tasks. I don't really have a specific time of day when it's worse since it feels pretty constant almost every day. However, the anxiety definitely peaks whenever I face any uncertainty or make even a minor mistake.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation where you need to concentrate, do you think your mind wanders because you're anxious about something, or do you feel like you're anxious because you're having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: My mind definitely wanders because worries suddenly intrude and disrupt my focus while I am trying to concentrate. It is not that I get anxious because I have trouble concentrating, but rather that these sudden, intense worries force their way in and make it impossible to stay on task.\n\nDoctor: So, to clarify, you feel that your anxiety and worries are the primary issue, and that they are causing your difficulties with concentration, rather than the other way around?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely, the worries are definitely the primary issue. My difficulty concentrating only happens because anxious thoughts suddenly intrude into my mind and completely disrupt my focus while I am trying to do other tasks.\n\nDoctor: It sounds like your symptoms are consistent with Generalized Anxiety Disorder. Would you like to discuss treatment options and ways to manage your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, that diagnosis makes a lot of sense, and I would definitely like to discuss treatment options. I really don't know how to manage these symptoms on my own, so I would appreciate any guidance you can provide."

Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.
